Uniform Estimates of Resolvents in Homogenization Theory of Elliptic Systems
Wei Wang

Abstract
. In this paper, we study the estimates of resolvents R ⁡ ( λ , ℒ ε ) = ( ℒ ε − λ ​ I ) − 1 R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})=(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)^{-1} , where ℒ ε = − div ( A ( x / ε ) ∇ ) \mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}=-\operatorname{div}(A(x/\varepsilon)\nabla) is a family of second elliptic operators with symmetric, periodic and oscillating coefficients defined on a bounded domain Ω \Omega with ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 . For 1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty , we will establish uniform L p → L p L^{p}\to L^{p} , L p → W 0 1 , p L^{p}\to W_{0}^{1,p} , W − 1 , p → L p W^{-1,p}\to L^{p} and W − 1 , p → W 0 1 , p W^{-1,p}\to W_{0}^{1,p} estimates by using the real variable method. Meanwhile, we use Green functions for operators ℒ ε − λ ​ I \mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I to study the asymptotic behavior of R ⁡ ( λ , ℒ ε ) R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}) and obtain convergence estimates in L p → L p L^{p}\to L^{p} , L p → W 0 1 , p L^{p}\to W_{0}^{1,p} norm. Keywords: Homogenization; Uniform estimates; Resolvents; Convergence.



1.
Introduction and main results

The main purpose of this paper is to study estimates of resolvents for a family of elliptic operators with rapidly oscillating and symmetric coefficients. Precisely speaking, we consider the resolvents of operators

(1.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}=-\operatorname{div}(A(x/\varepsilon)\nabla)=-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\left\{a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\left(\frac{x}{\varepsilon}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}\right\},\quad\varepsilon>0,

where
1\leq i,j\leq d
and
1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq m
. Here,
d\geq 2
denotes the dimension of Euclidean space and
m\geq 1
is the number of equations in the system. The summation convention for repeated indices is used throughout the paper. In discussion, we will always assume that the measurable matrix-valued functions
A(y)=(a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(y)):\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{m^{2}\times d^{2}}
satisfy the symmetry condition

(1.2)

\displaystyle a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(y)=a_{ji}^{\beta\alpha}(y)\text{ for any }1\leq i,j\leq d,1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq m\text{ and }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

the uniform ellipticity condition

(1.3)

\displaystyle\mu|\xi|^{2}\leq a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(y)\xi_{i}^{\alpha}\xi_{j}^{\beta}\leq\mu^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\text{ for any }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }\xi=(\xi_{i}^{\alpha})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d},

where
\mu>0
is a positive constant and the periodicity condition

(1.4)

\displaystyle A(y+z)=A(y)\text{ for any }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}.

To ensure
L^{p}
,
W^{1,p}
and Lipschitz estimates of operators
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
, in some situations, we need more smoothness conditions on the coefficients matrix
A
, i.e. the Hölder regularity of
A
,

(1.5)

\displaystyle|A(x)-A(y)|\leq\tau|x-y|^{\nu}\text{ for any }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
\tau>0,\nu\in(0,1)
and the
\VMO
condition (vanishing mean oscillation condition),

(1.6)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},0<\rho<t}\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B(x,\rho)}\left|A(y)-\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{B(x,\rho)}A\right|dy\leq\omega(t)\text{ and }\lim_{t\to 0}\omega(t)=0,

where
\omega(t)
is a continuous nondecreasing function. To make the notation simpler, we denote that

A\in\VMO(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if
A
satisfies the
\VMO
condition
(
1.6
)
.

Assume that
A(y)=(a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(y))
, the coefficient matrix of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}=-\operatorname{div}(A(x/\varepsilon)\nabla)
, satisfies
(
1.3
)
and
(
1.4
)
. Let
\chi_{j}^{\beta}(y)=(\chi_{j}^{\alpha\beta}(y))
denote the matrix of correctors for
\mathcal{L}_{1}=-\operatorname{div}(A(x)\nabla)
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\chi_{j}^{\beta}(y)=(\chi_{j}^{1\beta}(y),...,\chi_{j}^{m\beta}(y))\in H_{\operatorname{per}}^{1}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{m})
is defined by the following cell problem

(1.7)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &-\operatorname{div}(A(x)\nabla\chi_{j}^{\beta})=\operatorname{div}(A(x)\nabla P_{j}^{\beta})\text{ in }[0,1)^{d},\\
&\chi_{j}^{\beta}\text{ is periodic with respect to }\mathbb{Z}^{d}\text{ and }\int_{Y}\chi_{j}^{\beta}dy=0,\end{aligned}\right.

where
1\leq j\leq d
,
1\leq\beta\leq m
,
Y=[0,1)^{d}\cong\mathbb{R}^{d}/\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and
P_{j}^{\beta}=(P_{j}^{\alpha\beta}(x))=(x_{j}\delta^{\alpha\beta})
with
\delta^{\alpha\beta}=1
if
\alpha=\beta
,
\delta^{\alpha\beta}=0
otherwise. The homogenized operator is defined by
\mathcal{L}_{0}=-\operatorname{div}(\widehat{A}\nabla)
, where the coefficients
\widehat{A}=(\widehat{a}_{ij}^{\alpha\beta})
are given by

(1.8)

\displaystyle\widehat{a}_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}=\int_{Y}\left[a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(y)+a_{ik}^{\alpha\gamma}(y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{k}}\chi_{j}^{\gamma\beta}(y)\right]dy.

It is easy to show that if
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
, then
\widehat{A}
is also symmetric, that is

(1.9)

\displaystyle\widehat{a}_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}=\widehat{a}_{ji}^{\beta\alpha}\text{ for any }1\leq i,j\leq d\text{ and }1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq m.

Moreover, if
A
satisfies
(
1.3
)
, then there exists
\mu_{1}>0
depending only on
\mu
, such that

(1.10)

\displaystyle\mu_{1}|\xi|^{2}\leq\widehat{a}_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\xi_{i}^{\alpha}\xi_{j}^{\beta}\leq\mu_{1}^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\text{ for any }\xi=(\xi_{i}^{\alpha})\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}.

For the sake of simplicity, we set
\min(\mu,\mu_{1})
as new
\mu
in the rest of the paper. Let

(1.11)

\displaystyle b_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(y)=\widehat{a}_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}-a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(y)-a_{ik}^{\alpha\gamma}(y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{k}}\chi_{j}^{\gamma\beta}(y),

where
1\leq i,j\leq d
and
1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq m
. It is easy to see that
\int_{Y}b_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(y)dy=0
by
(
1.8
)
. Then in view of
(
1.7
)
and
(
1.8
)
, there exists the flux corrector
(F_{kij}^{\alpha\beta}(y))_{1\leq\alpha\leq m}\in H_{\operatorname{per}}^{1}(Y;\mathbb{R}^{m})
such that

(1.12)

\displaystyle b_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(y)=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{k}}\left\{F_{kij}^{\alpha\beta}(y)\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad F_{kij}^{\alpha\beta}(y)=-F_{ikj}^{\alpha\beta}(y)

for any
1\leq i,j,k\leq d
and
1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq m
. Moreover,
(F_{kij}^{\alpha\beta})\in L^{\infty}(Y)
if
(\chi_{j}^{\alpha\beta})
is Hölder continuous. For details about the proof of this fact, one can refer to Chaper 2 of
[
21
]
.

Let
A
satisfy
(
1.2
)
,
(
1.3
)
and
(
1.4
)
. For
F\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
\varepsilon\geq 0
, we can define a linear operator
T_{\varepsilon}:L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})\to H_{0}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})\subset L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
by
T_{\varepsilon}(F)=u_{\varepsilon}\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
such that
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=F
in
\Omega
and
u_{\varepsilon}=0
on
\partial\Omega
. Using standard arguments in
[
14
]
, operators
T_{\varepsilon}
with
\varepsilon\geq 0
are positive and self-adjoint. Therefore, applying the spectrum theory, it is natural to consider properties of resolvents
R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})=(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)^{-1}
with
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\backslash(0,\infty)
. To simplify notations, we will use
R(\lambda,\mathcal{L})
to denote the resolvent of the elliptic operator
\mathcal{L}
in the rest of the paper. In order to better characterize resolvents of operators, for
\lambda=|\lambda|e^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{C}\backslash(0,\infty)
, we define a constant
c(\lambda,\theta)
by

(1.13)

\displaystyle c(\lambda,\theta)=\left\{\begin{matrix}1&\text{if}&\theta\in[\pi/2,3\pi/2]\text{ or }\lambda=0\\
|\sin\theta|^{-1}&\text{if}&\theta\in(0,\pi/2)\cup(3\pi/2,2\pi)\text{ and }|\lambda|>0.\end{matrix}\right.

To present our results more conveniently and study the estimates of resolvents, we need to introduce the matrix of Dirichlet correctors
\Phi_{\varepsilon}(x)=(\Phi_{\varepsilon,j}^{\beta}(x))_{1\leq j\leq d,1\leq\beta\leq m}
in
\Omega
, defined by

(1.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\Phi_{\varepsilon,j}^{\beta}(x))=0\text{ in }\Omega\quad\text{and}\quad\Phi_{\varepsilon,j}^{\beta}(x)=P_{j}^{\beta}(x)\text{ on }\partial\Omega.

The Dirichlet correctors were first introduced in
[
2
]
to study the uniform Lipschitz estimates of homogenization problems. It is known that if
A
satisfies
(
1.3
)
,
(
1.4
)
,
(
1.5
)
and
\Omega
is a bounded
C^{1,\eta}

\eta\in(0,1)
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\geq 2
, then

(1.15)

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{\varepsilon,j}^{\beta}-P_{j}^{\beta}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C\varepsilon\quad\text{and}\quad\|\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq C,

where
C
depends only on
\mu,d,m,\tau,\nu,\eta
and
\Omega
. The following are the main results of this paper. For the sake of simplicity, we will denote
\diam(\Omega)
, the diameter of the bounded domain
\Omega
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\geq 2
by
R_{0}
throughout this paper.

Theorem 1.1

(
L^{2}
and
H_{0}^{1}
convergence of resolvents)
.

Suppose that
d\geq 2
,
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
,
(
1.3
)
,
(
1.4
)
and
(
1.5
)
. Let
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{1,1}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\lambda=|\lambda|e^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{C}\backslash(0,\infty)
. For
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
F\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
, let
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
be the unique solution of the Dirichlet problem
(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F
in
\Omega
and
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=0
on
\partial\Omega
. Then

(1.16)

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}-u_{0,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon c^{2}(\lambda,\theta)(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

(1.17)

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}-u_{0,\lambda}-(\Phi_{\varepsilon,j}^{\beta}-P_{j}^{\beta})\frac{\partial u_{0,\lambda}^{\beta}}{\partial x_{j}}\|_{H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon c^{2}(\lambda,\theta)\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

where
C
depends only on
\mu,d,m,\tau,\nu,\Omega
and
\Phi_{\varepsilon}
is given by
(
1.14
)
. In operator forms,

(1.18)

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})-R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{0})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon c^{2}(\lambda,\theta)(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(1.19)

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})-R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{0})-K_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)\to H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon c^{2}(\lambda,\theta),

where
K_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)
are the operator correctors given by the formula

(1.20)

\displaystyle K_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)=\{K_{\varepsilon}^{\alpha}(\lambda)\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq m}=\{(\Phi_{\varepsilon,j}^{\alpha\beta}(x)-P_{j}^{\alpha\beta}(x))\partial_{x_{j}}R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{0})^{\beta}\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq m}.

Theorem
1.1
is actually a quantitative result of the homogenization theory for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
. In fact, if
\Omega
is a bounded Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\geq 2
and
A
satisfies
\eqref{el},\eqref{pe}
, it can be shown that
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\to u_{0,\lambda}
weakly in
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and strongly in
L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
. For more details about the homogenization problems of elliptic systems, one can refer to
[
4
]
and
[
21
]
.

In
[
22
]
, assuming that
\Omega
is a bounded and
C^{1,1}
domain, the convergence rates of such resolvents are established, that is, for
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[0,\infty)
and
0\leq\varepsilon<1
,

(1.21)

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})-R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{0})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq Cc^{2}(\lambda,\theta)(\varepsilon|\lambda|^{-\frac{1}{2}}+\varepsilon^{2}),

(1.22)

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})-R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{0})-K_{0}(\varepsilon,\lambda)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)\to H^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq Cc^{2}(\lambda,\theta)\varepsilon^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
K_{0}(\varepsilon,\lambda)
is some corrector of the homogenization problem and
C
depends only on
\mu,d,m
and
\Omega
. These estimates are obtained by applying the results when
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
in
[
5
]
,
[
6
]
and some extension theorems. By using such approximation estimates for
R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})
,
[
16
]
and
[
17
]
gave the convergence rates for homogenization problems of parabolic and hyperbolic systems in
L^{2}
and
H_{0}^{1}
space. What is new for Theorem
1.1
is that we use different operator corectors and obtain a more brief proof under higher regularity assumptions of
A
. The main method is developed in the proof of Theorem 2.4 in
[
14
]
, which is used to deal with the case that
\lambda=0
. In Theorem 1.5 of
[
26
]
, the author generalized convergence results in
[
14
]
to elliptic operators with lower order terms. Such results are somewhat similar to this paper, but this does not mean that the conclusions of this paper can be trivially covered. To some extent, Theorem
1.1
is a generalization of Theorem 2.4 in
[
14
]
and Theorem 1.5 in
[
26
]
. The difference between this and Theorem 1.5 in
[
26
]
is that the constants on the right hand side of
(
1.16
)
do not depend on the module of
\lambda
, i.e.
|\lambda|
. Moreover, we remark that these estimates in Theorem
1.1
can also be applied to evolution systems and obtain similar results given in
[
12
]
,
[
16
]
and
[
17
]
.

Given a sectorial domain

(1.23)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}=\left\{\lambda=|\lambda|e^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{C}:|\lambda|>0,|\arg\theta|>\pi-\theta_{0}\right\},

where
\theta_{0}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, we can obtain the following estimates.

Theorem 1.2

(
L^{p}
and
W_{0}^{1,p}
estimates of resolvents)
.

Suppose that
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
d\geq 2
. Let
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}\cup\{0\}
with
\theta_{0}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
and
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{1}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Assume that
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
,
(
1.3
)
,
(
1.4
)
and
(
1.6
)
. Then for any
1<p<\infty
,
F\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
f\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times d})
, there exists a unique
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
such that
(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F+\operatorname{div}(f)
in
\Omega
,
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=0
on
\partial\Omega

and satisfies the uniform estimates

(1.24)

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\theta_{0}}(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-1}\|F\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+C_{p,\theta_{0}}(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},

(1.25)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\theta_{0}}(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|F\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+C_{p,\theta_{0}}\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},

where
C_{p,\theta_{0}}
depends only on
\mu,d,m,\omega(t),p,\theta_{0}
and
\Omega
. In operator forms,

(1.26)

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)\to L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\theta_{0}}(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-1},

(1.27)

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})\|_{W^{-1,p}(\Omega)\to L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\theta_{0}}(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(1.28)

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)\to W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\theta_{0}}(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(1.29)

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})\|_{W^{-1,p}(\Omega)\to W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\theta_{0}},

where
W^{-1,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})\triangleq(W_{0}^{1,p^{\prime}}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m}))^{*}
with
p^{\prime}=\frac{p}{p-1}
being the conjugate number of
p
.

The
L^{p}\to L^{p}
estimates of resolvents for elliptic operators are widely studied and have abundant materials. We will list some results for non-homogenization problems. For
m=1
and
\BMO
coefficients, see
[
13
]
; for constant coefficients and Dirichlet boundary conditions, see
[
18
]
; for constant coefficients and Neumann boundary conditions, see
[
25
]
; and for variable coefficients and Lipschitz domains, see
[
24
]
. It is noteworthy that in
[
24
]
, the authors derived the
L^{p}\to L^{p}
estimate of resolvents
R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{1})
without any regularity assumptions on
A
with
p
being closed to
2
when
\Omega
is a bounded Lipschitz domain. These results can be used for homogenization problems. In this point of view, Theorem
1.2
gives stronger results under more assumptions of
A
and
\Omega
. We see that the estimates established in Theorem
1.2
are sharp in view of the estimates for
p=2
, which will be given later.

The main tool for the proof of Theorem
1.2
is real variable method, which was original used in
[
7
]
to deal with
W^{1,p}
estimates of elliptic equations. We remark that for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
, the use of real variable method is slightly different from the case for
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
.

Besides the uniform
W^{-1,p},L^{p}\to L^{p},W_{0}^{1,p}
estimates of resolvents, one may also concern about the convergence rates in
L^{p}\to L^{p}
or
L^{p}\to W_{0}^{1,p}
norm with
1<p<\infty
. For these topics, we have the following theorems.

Theorem 1.3

(
L^{p}\to L^{p}
convergence rates of resolvents)
.

Suppose that
d\geq 2
,
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}\cup\{0\}
with
\theta_{0}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
and
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
,
(
1.3
)
,
(
1.4
)
,
(
1.5
)
. Let
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{1,1}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
F\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
, let
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
be the unique solution of the Dirichlet problem
(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F
in
\Omega
and
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=0
on
\partial\Omega
. Then for any
1<p<\infty
,

(1.30)

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}-u_{0,\lambda}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\theta_{0}}\varepsilon(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|F\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},

where
C_{p,\theta_{0}}
depends only on
\mu,d,m,\tau,\nu,p
and
\Omega
. In operator forms,

(1.31)

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})-R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{0})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)\to L^{p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\theta_{0}}\varepsilon(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Theorem 1.4

(
L^{p}\to W_{0}^{1,p}
convergence rates of resolvents)
.

Suppose that
d\geq 2
,
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}\cup\{0\}
with
\theta_{0}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
and
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
,
(
1.3
)
,
(
1.4
)
,
(
1.5
)
. Let
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{2,1}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
F\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
, let
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
be the unique solution of the Dirichlet problem
(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F
in
\Omega
and
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=0
on
\partial\Omega
. Then for any
1<p<\infty
,

(1.32)

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}-u_{0,\lambda}-(\Phi_{\varepsilon,j}^{\beta}-P_{j}^{\beta})\frac{\partial u_{0,\lambda}^{\beta}}{\partial x_{j}}\|_{W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\theta_{0}}\varepsilon\left\{\ln[\varepsilon^{-1}R_{0}+2]\right\}^{4|\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}|}\|F\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)},

where
C_{p,\theta_{0}}
depends only on
\mu,d,m,\tau,\nu,p
and
\Omega
. In operator forms,

(1.33)

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})-R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{0})-K_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)\to W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{p,\theta_{0}}\varepsilon\left\{\ln[\varepsilon^{-1}R_{0}+2]\right\}^{4|\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}|},

where
K_{\varepsilon}(\lambda)
is defined by
(
1.20
)
.

For the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
, the estimates of
L^{p}
and
W_{0}^{1,p}
convergence rates are established in
[
15
]
. These estimates are established by obtaining the convergence rates of Green functions of operators
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
. The existences and pointwise estimates of Green functions for operators
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
are well-known and one can refer to
[
11
]
and
[
23
]
for details. However, for operators
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
with
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}
and
\theta_{0}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
, the constructions and estimates of Green functions are still unknown. We will construct the Green functions for operators
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
in this paper and establish the uniform estimates of them. We point out that the constructions of Green functions for operators
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
with
d\geq 3
are similar to which of the Green functions of elliptic operators with lower order terms in
[
26
]
. There are some differences between these two. The first is that the problems considered in this paper are about complex valued functions. This makes calculations a little more complicated. The second difference is that the impact of the parameter
\lambda
should be calculated explicitly. On the other hand, for
d=2
, we need to employ arguments in
[
8
]
,
[
9
]
and
[
23
]
. The constructions of Green functions for
d\geq 3
and
d=2
are rather different and to deal with the case
d=2
, we need some properties of
\BMO
space and Hardy space. Moreover, proofs of uniform estimates on two dimensional Green functions are much more difficult than that of the case
d\geq 3
. These estimates are new and are the most important innovations of this paper. What is essential is that all the uniform regularity estimates of Green functions in this paper are scaling invariant (see
[
27
]
for such estimates of fundamental solutions related to generalized elliptic operators with lower order terms).

After constructing Green functions for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
, we will use almost the same methods in
[
15
]
to prove Theorem
1.3
-
1.4
. In view of the convergence rates with
p=2
, we can also see that estimates
(
1.31
)
and
(
1.33
)
are sharp.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we will give some basic ingredients in the proof, including the
L^{2}
regularity theory, Caccioppoli’s inequality and some properties about
\BMO
, Hardy space. In Section
3
, we will establish the
W^{1,p}
, Hölder and Lipschitz estimates for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
. In Section
4
, we will first prove Theorem
1.2
by using real variable methods. Then we will construct Green functions
G_{\varepsilon,\lambda}(x,y)
for operators
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
and obtain the regularity estimates for them. In Section
5
, firstly, we will use standard methods given in
[
14
]
to prove Theorem
1.1
. Next, we will calculate the convergence rates of Green functions for
(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)
. Using these tools, we can prove Theorem
1.3
-
1.4
.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Energy estimates and Caccioppoli’s inequality

A notable observation gives that for any
\xi=\xi^{(1)}+i\xi^{(2)}\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times d}
, where
\xi^{(1)},\xi^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
,

\displaystyle a_{kj}^{\alpha\beta}(y)\xi_{k}^{\alpha}\overline{\xi_{j}^{\beta}}

\displaystyle=a_{kj}^{\alpha\beta}(y)\left(\xi_{k}^{(1)\alpha}+i\xi_{k}^{(2)\alpha}\right)\left(\xi_{j}^{(1)\beta}-i\xi_{j}^{(2)\beta}\right)=a_{kj}^{\alpha\beta}(y)\xi_{k}^{(1)\alpha}\xi_{j}^{(1)\beta}+a_{kj}^{\alpha\beta}(y)\xi_{k}^{(2)\alpha}\xi_{j}^{(2)\beta}.

This, together with
(
1.3
)
, gives the ellipticity condition for
A
with complex variables, i.e.

(2.1)

\displaystyle 2\mu|\xi|^{2}\leq a_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}(y)\xi_{i}^{\alpha}\overline{\xi_{j}^{\beta}}\leq 2\mu^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\text{ for any }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }\xi=(\xi_{i}^{\alpha})\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times d}.

Similarly, in view of
(
1.9
)
and
(
1.10
)
, we can also infer that

(2.2)

\displaystyle 2\mu|\xi|^{2}\leq\widehat{a}_{ij}^{\alpha\beta}\xi_{i}^{\alpha}\overline{\xi_{j}^{\beta}}\leq 2\mu^{-1}|\xi|^{2}\text{ for any }y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\text{ and }\xi=(\xi_{i}^{\alpha})\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times d}.

Moreover, for
d\geq 2
, let
\Omega
be a bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\varepsilon\geq 0
,
\lambda=|\lambda|e^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{C}\backslash(0,\infty)
and
A
satisfy
\eqref{sy},\eqref{el}
. Define a bilinear form
B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[\cdot,\cdot]:H_{0}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})\times H_{0}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})\to\mathbb{C}
by

(2.3)

\displaystyle B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u,v]=\int_{\Omega}A_{\varepsilon}(x)\nabla u(x)\overline{\nabla v(x)}dx-\lambda\int_{\Omega}u(x)\overline{v(x)}dx,

where
A_{\varepsilon}(x)=A(x/\varepsilon)
for
\varepsilon>0
and
A_{0}(x)=\widehat{A}
. For
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[0,\infty)
and
u\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
,

(2.4)

\displaystyle B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u,u]=\int_{\Omega}A_{\varepsilon}(x)\nabla u(x)\overline{\nabla u(x)}dx-\lambda\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{2}dx.

If
\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)\geq 0
, we can take imaginary parts of both sides of
(
2.4
)
and obtain that

(2.5)

\displaystyle|B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u,u]|\geq|\operatorname{Im}(\lambda)|\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{2}dx\Rightarrow\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq c(\lambda,\theta)|\lambda|^{-1}|B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u,u]|.

If
\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)<0
, we can take real parts of both sides of
(
2.4
)
and get that

(2.6)

\displaystyle|B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u,u]|\geq|\operatorname{Re}(\lambda)|\int_{\Omega}|u(x)|^{2}dx.

Adding
(
2.6
)
by
(
2.5
)
, it can be easily shown that

(2.7)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)|\lambda|^{-1}|B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u,u]|\text{ for any }\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\backslash[0,\infty).

This, together with
(
2.1
)
,
(
2.2
)
and
(
2.4
)
, implies that

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mu\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq|B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u,u]|+|\lambda|\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq 2c(\lambda,\theta)|B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u,u]|.

If
\lambda=0
, it is obvious that
(
2.8
)
is still true. By using Poincaré’s inequality, then

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq CR_{0}^{2}\|\nabla u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)R_{0}^{2}|B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u,u]|,

which, together with
(
2.7
)
, implies that for any
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\backslash(0,\infty)
,

(2.9)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-1}|B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u,u]|,

where
C
depends only on
\mu,d,m
and
\Omega
. Another important fact is that if
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
, the adjoint operator of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
is
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\lambda}I
. Indeed,

\displaystyle\langle(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u),v\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Omega)\times H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle=B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u,v]=\overline{B_{\varepsilon,\overline{\lambda},\Omega}[v,u]}=\langle u,(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\overline{\lambda}I)(v)\rangle_{H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)\times H^{-1}(\Omega)}.

In view of
(
2.8
)
and well-known Lax-Milgram theorem, it is easy to show the following theorem for existence of solutions corresponding to operators
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{1}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\geq 2
. Assume that
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
and
(
1.3
)
. Then for any
\varepsilon\geq 0
,
F\in H^{-1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
, there is a unique weak solution
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
satisfying the Dirichlet problem
(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F
in
\Omega
and
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=0
on
\partial\Omega
. That is, for any
\varphi\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
,

\displaystyle B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u_{\varepsilon,\lambda},\varphi]=\langle F,\varphi\rangle_{H^{-1}(\Omega)\times H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)}.

Lemma 2.2
.

Suppose that
d\geq 2
and
\lambda=|\lambda|e^{i\theta}\in\mathbb{C}\backslash(0,\infty)
. Let
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{1}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Assume that
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
and
(
1.3
)
. Then for any
\varepsilon\geq 0
,
F\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
f\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times d})
, there exists a unique weak solution
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in H_{0}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
of the Dirichlet problem
(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F+\operatorname{div}(f)
in
\Omega
and
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=0
on
\partial\Omega
, satisfying the uniform energy estimates

(2.10)

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)\left\{(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-1}\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right\},

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)\left\{(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right\},

where
C
depends only on
\mu,d,m
and
\Omega
. Moreover, if
\Omega
is a bounded
C^{1,1}
domain,
\varepsilon=0
and
f\equiv 0
, then

(2.11)

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}u_{0,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

In operator forms,

(2.12)

\displaystyle\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-1},

(2.13)

\displaystyle\max\left\{\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})\|_{H^{-1}(\Omega)\to L^{2}(\Omega)},\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)\to H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\max\left\{\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{0})\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)\to H^{2}(\Omega)},\|R(\lambda,\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon})\|_{H^{-1}(\Omega)\to H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta).

Proof.

The existence is ensured by Theorem
2.1
, we only need to show
(
2.10
)
and
(
2.11
)
. Firstly, we can choose
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}
and
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}
in
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
such that

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{matrix}(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)})=F&\text{in}&\Omega,\\
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}=0&\text{on}&\partial\Omega,\end{matrix}\right.\quad\text{and}\quad\left\{\begin{matrix}(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)})=\operatorname{div}(f)&\text{in}&\Omega,\\
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}=0&\text{on}&\partial\Omega.\end{matrix}\right.

The next thing is to estimate
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}
and
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}
respectively. For
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}
, we need to prove that

(2.15)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Choosing
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}
as the test function, it can be obtained that

(2.16)

\displaystyle B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)},u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}]=\int_{\Omega}F(x)\overline{u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}(x)}dx=(F,u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)})_{L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega)}.

In view of
(
2.8
)
and
(
2.9
)
, we have

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-1}\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle\mu\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

which complete the proof of
(
2.15
)
. For
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}
, it suffices to show that

(2.17)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Choosing
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}
as the test function, then

(2.18)

\displaystyle B_{\varepsilon,\lambda,\Omega}[u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)},u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}]=-\int_{\Omega}f(x)\overline{\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}(x)}dx=-(f,\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)})_{L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega)}.

Again, by applying
(
2.8
)
and
(
2.9
)
, it is easy to get that

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-1}\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

\displaystyle\mu\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(2)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

which implies
(
2.17
)
. At last, for the proof of
(
2.11
)
, since
\Omega
is
C^{1,1}
, one can deduce that

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}u_{0,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\{\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+|\lambda|\|u_{0,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right\}\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)\|F\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)},

where, for the first inequality, we have used standard
H^{2}
estimates for elliptic systems with constant coefficients and for the second inequality, we have used
(
2.10
)
.
∎

Remark 2.3
.

For
d\geq 3
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}\backslash(0,\infty)
, using Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev embedding theorem
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)\subset L^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}(\Omega)
, we have

\displaystyle|(F,u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)})_{L^{2}(\Omega)\times L^{2}(\Omega)}|\leq\|F\|_{L^{\frac{2d}{d+2}}(\Omega)}\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}\|_{L^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}(\Omega)}\leq\|F\|_{L^{\frac{2d}{d+2}}(\Omega)}\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}^{(1)}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}.

By using the same arguments in the proof of Theorem
2.2
, it is easy to find that for any
\varepsilon\geq 0
,

(2.19)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)\left\{\|F\|_{L^{\frac{2d}{d+2}}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right\},

where
C
depends only on
\mu,d,m
and
\Omega
. Similarly, If
d=2
, in view of the Sobolev embedding
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega)\subset L^{q^{\prime}}(\Omega)
with
q^{\prime}=\frac{q}{q-1}>1
, it can be easily seen that for any
q>1
,

(2.20)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq Cc(\lambda,\theta)\left\{R_{0}^{2(1-\frac{1}{q})}\|F\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}+\|f\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right\},

where
C
depends only on
\mu,m,q
and
\Omega
.

Next, we will establish the Caccioppoli’s inequality for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
. In some point of view, Caccioppoli’s inequality can be seen as the localization of
(
2.19
)
. In this paper, we need to obtain Caccioppoli’s inequality that is scaling invariant. This means that
\lambda
cannot be regarded as a constant and its influence on the constants of this inequality should be calculated explicitly. The scaling invariant Caccioppoli’s inequality plays a vital role in the proofs of other scaling invariant inequalities. The idea comes from
[
18
,
27
]
and we combine the methods in both of them to complete the proof for the sake of completeness. To simplify the notations, we can define

(2.21)

\displaystyle\Omega(x_{0},R)=\Omega\cap B(x_{0},R)\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta(x_{0},R)=\partial\Omega\cap B(x_{0},R)

for any
0<R<R_{0}
and
x_{0}\in\Omega
. Sometimes we will use
\Omega_{r}
and
\Delta_{r}
to denote
\Omega(x_{0},r)
and
\Delta(x_{0},r)
if no confusion would be made.

Lemma 2.4

(Caccioppoli’s inequality)
.

Let
\varepsilon\geq 0
,
d\geq 2
,
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}\cup\{0\}
with
\theta_{0}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
and
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{1}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Assume that
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
and
(
1.3
)
. Suppose that
0<R<R_{0}
,
x_{0}\in\Omega
,
f\in L^{2}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
F\in L^{q}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
, where
q=\frac{2d}{d+2}
if
d\geq 3
and
q>1
if
d=2
. If
\Delta(x_{0},2R)\neq\emptyset
, assume that
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in H^{1}(\Omega(x_{0},2R),\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the weak solution of the boundary problem

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F+\operatorname{div}(f)\text{ in }\Omega(x_{0},2R)\quad\text{and}\quad u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=0\text{ on }\Delta(x_{0},2R).

If
\Delta(x_{0},2R)=\emptyset
, assume that
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in H^{1}(B(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the weak solution of the interior problem

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F+\operatorname{div}(f)\text{ in }B(x_{0},2R).

Then for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

(2.22)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},R)}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k,\theta_{0}}}{(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{k}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{C_{k,\theta_{0}}R}{(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\{R\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|F|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|f|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\},

(2.23)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},R)}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k,\theta_{0}}}{(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{k}R}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+C_{k,\theta_{0}}\left\{R\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|F|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|f|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\},

where
C_{k,\theta_{0}}
depends only on
\mu,d,m,q,k,\theta_{0},\Omega
.

Proof.

We will only prove the case for
d\geq 3
, since the case
d=2
follows from almost the same methods by substituting the Sobolev embedding theorem
H_{0}^{1}\subset L^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}
with
H_{0}^{1}\subset L^{q^{\prime}}
,
1<q<\infty
. Firstly, we can assume that
R=1
and
x_{0}=0
and for general case,
(
2.22
)
and
(
2.23
)
follows by rescaling and translation. For
\lambda=0
, the results are obvious by standard Caccioppoli’s inequality for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
. Then we can assume that
\lambda\neq 0
. Choose
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B(0,2);\mathbb{R})
such that
\varphi\equiv 1
for
x\in B(0,1)
,
\varphi\equiv 0
for
x\in B(0,\frac{3}{2})^{c}
,
|\nabla\varphi|\leq C
and
0\leq\varphi\leq 1
. We can set
\psi=\varphi^{2}u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}
as the test function. By applying the definition of weak solutions, we have

(2.24)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{2}}A_{\varepsilon}\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\overline{\nabla(\varphi^{2}u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})}-\lambda\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi^{2}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}=\int_{\Omega_{2}}F\overline{\varphi^{2}u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}}-\int_{\Omega_{2}}f\overline{\nabla(\varphi^{2}u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})},

where
A_{\varepsilon}=A(x/\varepsilon)
if
\varepsilon>0
and
A_{0}=\widehat{A}
. In the following calculations, we use
C_{\theta_{0}}
to denote a constant depending on
\theta_{0}
and
C_{k,\theta_{0}}
a constant depending on
\theta_{0}
and
k
. If
\operatorname{Re}\lambda\geq 0
, we can take the imaginary parts of both sides of
(
2.24
)
, then

(2.25)

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi^{2}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\{\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}||\nabla\varphi||u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|+\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi^{2}|F||u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|+\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi|f|\left(|\nabla\varphi||u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|+\varphi|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|\right)\right\}.

Noticing that
|\lambda|=c(\lambda,\theta)|\operatorname{Im}\lambda|
, it actually means that

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi^{2}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\theta_{0}}}{|\lambda|}\left\{\int_{\Omega_{2}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}+\delta\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi^{2}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}dx+\int_{\Omega_{2}}|\varphi F||\varphi u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|+\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi^{2}|f|^{2}\right\},

where we have used Cauchy’s inequality and the following inequality

(2.26)

\displaystyle ab\leq\delta a^{2}+\frac{1}{4\delta}b^{2}\text{ for any }\delta>0\text{ and }a,b>0,

with
\delta>0
being sufficiently small. By using Hölder’s inequality, it is easy to see that

(2.27)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi^{2}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\theta_{0}}}{|\lambda|}\left\{\int_{\Omega_{2}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}+\delta\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi^{2}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|\varphi F|^{\frac{2d}{d+2}}\right)^{\frac{d+2}{2d}}\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|\varphi u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}\right)^{\frac{d-2}{2d}}+\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi^{2}|f|^{2}\right\}.

If
\operatorname{Re}\lambda<0
, we can take real parts of both sides of
(
2.24
)
and conclude that

(2.28)

\displaystyle|\operatorname{Re}\lambda|\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi^{2}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\{\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}||\nabla\varphi||u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|+\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi^{2}|F||u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|+\int_{\Omega_{2}}\varphi|f|\left(|\nabla\varphi||u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|+\varphi|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|\right)\right\}.

By adding
(
2.25
)
to
(
2.28
)
, we can also derive
(
2.27
)
for the case
\operatorname{Re}\lambda<0
. Then
(
2.27
)
is true for any
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}
. Owing to
H_{0}^{1}(\Omega_{2})\subset L^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}(\Omega_{2})
and
(
2.26
)
, it can be seen that

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|\varphi F|^{\frac{2d}{d+2}}\right)^{\frac{d+2}{2d}}\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|\varphi u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}\right)^{\frac{d-2}{2d}}\leq C\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|\varphi F|^{\frac{2d}{d+2}}\right)^{\frac{d+2}{d}}+\delta\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|\varphi u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{\frac{2d}{d-2}}\right)^{\frac{d-2}{d}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\leq C\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|\varphi F|^{\frac{2d}{d+2}}\right)^{\frac{d+2}{d}}+\delta\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}(|\nabla\varphi u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|+|\varphi\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

This, together with
(
2.27
)
and the properties of
\varphi
, leads to

(2.29)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\Omega_{1}}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{C_{\theta_{0}}}{|\lambda|^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\{\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|F|^{\frac{2d}{d+2}}\right)^{\frac{d+2}{2d}}+\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|f|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}.

Combining
(
2.24
)
and
(
2.29
)
, we have

(2.30)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\Omega_{1}}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C_{\theta_{0}}\left\{\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|F|^{\frac{2d}{d+2}}\right)^{\frac{d+2}{2d}}+\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|f|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}.

In view of the obvious fact that
\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{1})}\leq C\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{2})}
, we can deduce from
(
2.29
)
that for any
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\Omega_{1}}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{C_{\theta_{0}}}{(1+|\lambda|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left\{\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|F|^{\frac{2d}{d+2}}\right)^{\frac{d+2}{2d}}+\left(\int_{\Omega_{2}}|f|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}.

This, together with
(
2.30
)
, gives the proof by repeating the procedure for
2k
times.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

Choose
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B(0,2R);\mathbb{R})
such that
\varphi\equiv 1
for
x\in B(x_{0},R)
,
\varphi\equiv 0
for
x\in B(x_{0},\frac{3}{2}R)^{c}
,
|\nabla\varphi|\leq C/R
and
0\leq\varphi\leq 1
. According to
(
2.25
)
,
(
2.26
)
and
(
2.28
)
, it can be easily seen that, if
\lambda\neq 0
,
f\equiv 0
and
F\equiv 0
, then

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}\varphi^{2}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\theta_{0}}}{|\lambda|}\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}\varphi|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}||\nabla\varphi||u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\theta_{0}}}{|\lambda|^{2}}\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}\varphi^{2}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2},

which, together with the properties of
\varphi
, implies that

(2.31)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},R)}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{C_{\theta_{0}}}{|\lambda|}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\frac{C_{\theta_{0}}}{|\lambda|R}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

2.2.
\operatorname{BMO}
and Hardy space

To deal with the Green functions with
d=2
, we will need some basic knowledge on real analysis, mainly about
\BMO
space and Hardy space.

Definition 2.6

(
\operatorname{BMO}
space and atom functions)
.

Let
d\geq 2
. Assume that
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
r>0
and
\Omega
is a bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Denote
\Omega(x_{0},r)=\Omega\cap B(x_{0},r)
as before.
\operatorname{BMO}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
is a space containing all measurable,
\mathbb{C}^{m}
-valued functions such that

(2.32)

\displaystyle\left\|u\right\|_{\BMO(\Omega)}=\sup\left\{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},r)}|u-u_{x_{0},r}|:x_{0}\in\overline{\Omega},r>0\right\}<\infty,

where

\displaystyle u_{x_{0},r}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&\text{ if }&r\geq\delta(x_{0})=\operatorname{dist}(x_{0},\partial\Omega),\\
\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},r)}u&\text{ if }&r<\delta(x_{0})=\operatorname{dist}(x_{0},\partial\Omega).\end{array}\right.

We call a bounded measurable function
a(x)
as an atom function in
\Omega
if
\operatorname{supp}(a)\subset\Omega(x_{0},r)
with
x_{0}\in\overline{\Omega}
,
0<r<R_{0}
and

\displaystyle\left\|a\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\leq\frac{1}{|\Omega(x_{0},r)|},\quad a_{x_{0},r}=0.

Definition 2.7

(Hardy space)
.

Let
\Omega
be a
C^{1}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\geq 2
. A function
f
is an element in the Hardy space
\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
, if there exist a sequence of atoms
\left\{a_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
and a sequence of complex numbers
\left\{\eta_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\subset l^{1}(\mathbb{C})
, such that
f(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\eta_{i}a_{i}(x)
. We define the norm in this space by

\displaystyle\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(\Omega)}=\inf\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}|\eta_{i}|:f(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\eta_{i}a_{i}(x)\right\}.

We notice the expression

\displaystyle\sup\left\{\int_{\Omega}a(y)u(y)dy:a=a(x)\text{ is an atom in }\Omega\right\},

gives the equivalent norm of
\operatorname{BMO}(\Omega)
. This is because that
\operatorname{BMO}(\Omega)
is the dual space of
\mathcal{H}^{1}
.

3.
Uniform regularity estimates

It is well-known that for the homogenization problem with real vector values, the
W^{1,p}
estimates for
u_{\varepsilon}
are established (see
[
2
]
and
[
20
]
). By simple observations, we can obtain similar results for the problem with complex vector values.

Lemma 3.1

(
W^{1,p}
estimates for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
)
.

For
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
d\geq 2
, let
2<p<\infty
and
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{1}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Suppose that
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
,
(
1.3
)
,
(
1.4
)
and
(
1.6
)
. Assume that
f=(f_{i}^{\alpha})\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times d})
and
F\in L^{q}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
with
q=\frac{pd}{p+d}
. Then the weak solution of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=F+\operatorname{div}(f)
in
\Omega
and
u_{\varepsilon}=0
on
\partial\Omega
satisfies the uniform estimate

(3.1)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\{\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}+\|F\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}\right\},

where
C
depends only on
\mu,d,m,p,q,\omega(t)
and
\Omega
.

Proof.

For the case that
f\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{m\times d})
and
F\in L^{q}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{m})
, the proof is trivial. For
f\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times d})
and
F\in L^{q}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
, we can write that
f=g+ih
and
F=H+iG
with
g,h\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{m\times d})
,
G,H\in L^{q}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{m})
and use the
W^{1,p}
estimates for the case of real functions.
∎

Theorem 3.2

(Localization of
W^{1,p}
estimates for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
)
.

For
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
d\geq 2
, let
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}\cup\{0\}
with
\theta_{0}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
,
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{1}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
2<p<\infty
. Suppose that
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
,
(
1.3
)
,
(
1.4
)
and
(
1.6
)
. Assume that
x_{0}\in\Omega
,
0<R<R_{0}
,
f\in L^{p}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m\times d})
,
F\in L^{q}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
q=\frac{pd}{p+d}
. If
\Delta(x_{0},2R)\neq\emptyset
, assume that
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in H^{1}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the weak solution of the boundary problem

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F+\operatorname{div}(f)\text{ in }\Omega(x_{0},2R)\quad\text{and}\quad u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=0\text{ on }\Delta(x_{0},2R).

If
\Delta(x_{0},2R)=\emptyset
, assume that
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in H^{1}(B(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the weak solution of the interior problem

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F+\operatorname{div}(f)\text{ in }B(x_{0},2R).

Then there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, a constant integer depending only on
d
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},R)}|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k,\theta_{0}}}{(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{k}R}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+C_{k,\theta_{0}}(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{n}\left\{R\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|F|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|f|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},R)}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k,\theta_{0}}}{(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{k}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+C_{k,\theta_{0}}(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{n}\left\{R^{2}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|F|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+R\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|f|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\},

where
C_{k,\theta_{0}}
depends only on
\mu,d,m,k,\theta_{0},p,\omega(t)
and
\Omega
.

Proof.

By rescaling and translation, it can be assumed that
R=1
and
x_{0}=0
. We can choose
\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(B(0,2);\mathbb{R})
such that
0\leq\varphi\leq 1
,
\varphi\equiv 1
in
B(0,1)
,
\varphi\equiv 0
in
B(0,\frac{3}{2})^{c}
and
|\nabla\varphi|\leq C
. Then by setting
A_{\varepsilon}
as
A(x/\varepsilon)
if
\varepsilon>0
and
\widehat{A}
if
\varepsilon=0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F_{\varepsilon}+\operatorname{div}(f_{\varepsilon})\text{ in }\Omega_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\varphi u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=0\text{ on }\partial(\Omega_{2}),

where
F_{\varepsilon},f_{\varepsilon}
are defined by

\displaystyle F_{\varepsilon}=\lambda\varphi u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}+\varphi F-f\nabla\varphi-A_{\varepsilon}\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\nabla\varphi\quad\text{and}\quad f_{\varepsilon}=\varphi f-A_{\varepsilon}\nabla\varphi u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}.

Then owing to
(
3.1
)
and Hölder’s inequality, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\nabla(\varphi u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{3/2})}\leq C\left\{\|F_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{3/2})}+\|f_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{3/2})}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\leq C\left\{|\lambda|\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{3/2})}+\|F\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{3/2})}+\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{3/2})}+\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{3/2})}+\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{3/2})}\right\},

where
q=\frac{pd}{p+d}
. By using Hölder’s inequality again, this implies that

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\{(1+|\lambda|)\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{3/2})}+\|F\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{3/2})}+\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{3/2})}+\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{3/2})}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C\left\{(1+|\lambda|)\left(\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{2})}+\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{2})}\right)+\|F\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{2})}+\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{2})}\right\}.

Here, we have used the inequality that for any
2<p<\infty
and
u\in W^{1,q}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
with
q=\frac{pd}{p+d}
,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\leq C\|u\|_{W^{1,q}(\Omega)}\leq C\left\{\|\nabla u\|_{L^{q}(\Omega)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\right\},

where
C
depends only on
p,d,m,\Omega
. One can refer to
[
1
]
for details about
(
3.4
)
. By iterating for finite times (depending only on
d
), we can get
n=n(d)\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, such that

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{1})}\leq C(1+|\lambda|)^{n}\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{2})}+C(1+|\lambda|)^{n-1}\left\{\|F\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{2})}+\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{2})}\right\}.

In view of
(
2.22
)
, this yields that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{1})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k,\theta_{0}}(1+|\lambda|)^{n}}{(1+|\lambda|)^{k+n}}\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{2})}+C_{k,\theta_{0}}(1+|\lambda|)^{n}\left\{\|F\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{2})}+\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{2})}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{k,\theta_{0}}(1+|\lambda|)^{-k}\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(\Omega_{2})}+C_{k,\theta_{0}}(1+|\lambda|)^{n}\left\{\|F\|_{L^{q}(\Omega_{2})}+\|f\|_{L^{p}(\Omega_{2})}\right\}.

This inequality implies
(
3.2
)
. On the other hand,
(
3.3
)
follows from Poincaré’s inequality and the same arguments of localization.
∎

By using Sobolev embedding theorem and convex arguments (see
[
19
]
), we can use
W^{1,p}
estimates to obtain the Hölder and
L^{\infty}
estimates as follows. The proofs are trivial and for more details, one can refer to
[
26
]
.

Corollary 3.3

(Localization of Hölder and
L^{\infty}
estimates for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
)
.

For
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
d\geq 2
, let
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}\cup\{0\}
with
\theta_{0}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
and
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{1}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Suppose that
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
,
(
1.3
)
,
(
1.4
)
and
(
1.6
)
. Assume that
x_{0}\in\Omega
,
0<R<R_{0}
,
f\in L^{p}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m\times d})
,
F\in L^{q}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
q=\frac{pd}{p+d}
. If
\Delta(x_{0},2R)\neq\emptyset
, assume that
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in H^{1}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the weak solution of the boundary problem

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F+\operatorname{div}(f)\text{ in }\Omega(x_{0},2R)\quad\text{and}\quad u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=0\text{ on }\Delta(x_{0},2R).

If
\Delta(x_{0},2R)=\emptyset
, assume that
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in H^{1}(B(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the weak solution of the interior problem

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F+\operatorname{div}(f)\text{ in }B(x_{0},2R).

Then there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, a constant integer depending only on
d
, such that for
\gamma=1-\frac{d}{p}
, any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
and
0<s<\infty
,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\left[u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\right]_{C^{0,\gamma}(\Omega(x_{0},R))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k,\theta_{0}}}{(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{k}R^{\gamma}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{C_{k,\theta_{0}}(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{n}R}{R^{\gamma}}\left\{R\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|F|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|f|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\},

(3.6)

\displaystyle\|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega(x_{0},R))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k,\theta_{0}}}{(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{k}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{s}\right)^{\frac{1}{s}}

\displaystyle+C_{k,\theta_{0}}(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{n}\left\{R^{2}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|F|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+R\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|f|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\},

where
C_{k,\theta_{0}}
depends only on
\mu,d,m,k,\theta_{0},p,s,\omega(t)
and
\Omega
.

Theorem 3.4

(Localization of Lipschitz estimates for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
)
.

For
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
d\geq 2
, let
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{1,\eta}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
0<\eta<1
. Suppose that
A
satisfies
(
1.3
)
,
(
1.4
)
and
(
1.5
)
. Assume that
x_{0}\in\Omega
,
0<R<R_{0}
and
F\in L^{p}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
with
p>d
. If
\Delta(x_{0},2R)\neq\emptyset
, assume that
u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the weak solution of the boundary problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=F\text{ in }\Omega(x_{0},2R)\quad\text{and}\quad u_{\varepsilon}=0\text{ on }\Delta(x_{0},2R).

If
\Delta(x_{0},2R)=\emptyset
, assume that
u_{\varepsilon}\in H^{1}(B(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the weak solution of the interior problem

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon})=F\text{ in }B(x_{0},2R).

then

(3.7)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega(x_{0},R))}\leq\frac{C}{R}\left\{\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|u_{\varepsilon}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+R^{2}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|F|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\},

where
C
depends only on
\mu,d,m,\tau,\nu,\eta
and
\Omega
.

Proof.

See
[
3
]
or Theorem 3.1.1 and Theorem 4.5.1 of
[
21
]
.
∎

Theorem 3.5

(Localization of Lipschitz estimates for the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
)
.

For
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
d\geq 2
, Let
\lambda\in\Sigma_{\theta_{0}}\cup\{0\}
with
\theta_{0}\in(0,\frac{\pi}{2})
and
\Omega
be a bounded
C^{1,\eta}
domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
0<\eta<1
. Suppose that
A
satisfies
(
1.2
)
,
(
1.3
)
,
(
1.4
)
and
(
1.5
)
. Assume that
x_{0}\in\Omega
,
0<R<R_{0}
and
F\in L^{p}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
with
p>d
. If
\Delta(x_{0},2R)\neq\emptyset
, assume that
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in H^{1}(\Omega(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the weak solution of the boundary problem

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F\text{ in }\Omega(x_{0},2R)\quad\text{and}\quad u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=0\text{ on }\Delta(x_{0},2R).

If
\Delta(x_{0},2R)=\emptyset
, assume that
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\in H^{1}(B(x_{0},2R);\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the weak solution of the interior problem

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda})=F\text{ in }B(x_{0},2R).

Then there exists
n\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
, a constant integer depending only on
d
, such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega(x_{0},R))}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k,\theta_{0}}}{(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{k}R}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+C_{k,\theta_{0}}(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{n}R\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega(x_{0},2R)}|F|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},

where
C_{k,\theta_{0}}
depends only on
\mu,d,m,k,\theta_{0},\tau,\nu,\eta
and
\Omega
.

Proof.

In view of
(
3.4
)
, we can infer that

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{R}\left\{\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{3/2R}}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+R^{2}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{3/2R}}|F|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+|\lambda|R^{2}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{3/2R}}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\}.

By applying
(
3.3
)
to
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}
for index
p
, this yields that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega_{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{R}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{3/2R}}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+CR\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{3/2R}}|F|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{C_{k,\theta_{0}}}{(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{k-1}}\left\{\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{2R}}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+(1+|\lambda|R^{2})^{k+n}R^{2}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega_{2R}}|F|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\}.

This, together with
(
2.22
)
, implies
(
3.8
)
.
∎

4.
Green functions of operators

4.1.
Proof of Theorem
1.2
and relevant estimates

To begin with, we will use the well-known real variable method to prove Theorem
1.2
. Unlike what had been done in the proof of
W^{1,p}
estimates for
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
, the operators
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I
do not have the homogeneous property, that is, if
\lambda\neq 0
and
0\neq c\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
is a constant vector,
(\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}-\lambda I)(c)\neq 0
. For this reason, we need to make some adjustments for the original method employed on
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
. For simplicity, we use
B=B(x,r)
to denote a ball in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
d\geq 2
) and
tB=B(x,tr)
(
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
) to denote balls with center
x
and radius
tr
if no confusion would be caused.

Theorem 4.1

(Real variable method)
.

Let
q>2
and
\Omega
be a bounded Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\geq 2
. Let
F\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
f\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times d})
for some
2<p<q
. Suppose that for each ball
B
with the property that
|B|\leq c_{0}|\Omega|
and either
4B\subset\Omega
or
B
is centered on
\partial\Omega
, there exist two measurable functions
F_{B}
and
R_{B}
on
\Omega\cap 2B
, such that
|F|\leq|F_{B}|+|R_{B}|
on
\Omega\cap 2B
,

(4.1)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega\cap 2B}|R_{B}|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq N_{1}\left\{\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega\cap 4B}|F|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\sup_{4B_{0}\supset B^{\prime}\supset B}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega\cap B^{\prime}}|f|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\},

(4.2)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega\cap 2B}|F_{B}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq N_{2}\sup_{4B_{0}\supset B^{\prime}\supset B}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega\cap B^{\prime}}|f|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\eta\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega\cap 4B}|F|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
N_{1},N_{2}>0
and
0<c_{0}<1
. Then there exists
\eta_{0}>0
, depending only on
N_{1},N_{2},c_{0}
,
p,q
and the Lipschitz character of
\Omega
, with the property that if
0\leq\eta<\eta_{0}
, then
F\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and

(4.3)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}|F|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C\left\{\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}|F|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{\Omega}|f|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right\},

where
C
depends at most on
N_{1},N_{2},c_{0},p,q
and the Lipschitz character of
\Omega
.

Proof.

It is proved for the case that
F\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
f\in L^{p}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{m})
in Theorem 3.2.6 of
[
21
]
. It is easy to generalize it to the complex case through almost the same arguments.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Firstly, we assume that
\lambda\neq 0
, since the case
\lambda=0
is trivial in view of the results for
W^{1,p}
estimates of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}
. If
p=2
, the results are given by Lemma
2.2
. For
2<p<\infty
, choose
q=p+1
. Consider functions

\displaystyle H=|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|,\,\,h=(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-1}|F|+(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}|f|,\,\,G=|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|

\displaystyle\quad\quad\text{and }g=(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}|F|+|f|.

Now we will apply Theorem
4.1
to complete the proof. For each ball
B
with the property that
|B|\leq\frac{1}{100}|\Omega|
and
4B\subset\Omega
, we write
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}=u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,1}+u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,2}
in
2B
, where
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,1}\in H_{0}^{1}(4B;\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the weak solution of
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,1})-\lambda u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,1}=F+\operatorname{div}(f)
in
4B
and
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,1}=0
on
\partial(4B)
. Let

\displaystyle H_{B}=|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,1}|,\,\,R_{B}=|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,2}|,\,\,G_{B}=|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,1}|\text{ and }T_{B}=|\nabla u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,2}|.

Obviously,
|H|\leq H_{B}+R_{B}
and
|G|\leq G_{B}+T_{B}
in
2B
. It follows from Lemma
2.2
that

(4.4)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|H_{B}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\theta_{0}}}{r^{-2}+|\lambda|}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|F|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\frac{C_{\theta_{0}}}{(r^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|f|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\theta_{0}}\left\{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}\left(\frac{|F|+(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{\frac{1}{2}}|f|}{R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|}\right)^{2}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C_{\theta_{0}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|h|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

and

(4.5)

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|G_{B}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\theta_{0}}}{(r^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|F|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+C_{\theta_{0}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|f|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\theta_{0}}\left\{\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}\left(\frac{|F|}{(R_{0}^{-2}+|\lambda|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}+|f|\right)^{2}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C_{\theta_{0}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|g|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
r
is the radius of
B
. Moreover, we note that
u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,2}\in H^{1}(4B;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
\mathcal{L}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,2})-\lambda u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,2}=0
in
4B
. Owing to
(
3.3
)
, (
4.4
) and Lemma
2.2
, we can obtain that

\displaystyle\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{2B}|R_{B}|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\theta_{0}}\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,2}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C_{\theta_{0}}\left\{\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|u_{\varepsilon,\lambda,1}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\theta_{0}}\left\{\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|H|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$}}\kern-4.86108pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$}}\kern-3.43057pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.908pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}}\kern-2.76045pt}}\!\int_{4B}|h|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}.

To estimate
T_{B}
, we first note that
